1. Transcribe the entire contents of the text (the transcript of the game).

Now the main challenge: reconstructing all commands. Let me think about the exact approach.

The OCR at 1fps catches partial commands: e.g., ">n." (typing n then enter, got ">n" then response). The commands appear as ">" followed by textches.

Better: the transcript includes all commands. If we OCR every frame at 30 fps, we get the visible text buffer at each frame. Since the terminal scrolls, at many frames, the bottom of the screen contains the latest command that was just entered, and it stays visible for a while? But with a lot of output (room descriptions), the command line might scroll up quickly.

However, each command is followed by the game's response, and the response typically stays on screen for some time. The key is that the player types quickly)Skip.

Alternative: We could simulate the game? That's not feasible.

Alternative idea: Look at the video at the pixel level. The command line appears at a specific location. When the player types a command, the text appears near the bottom prompt ">". We can use OCR on each frame from a high-fps sampling to capture the prompt texts.

The cleanest: extract frames at original 30fps (5700 frames), OCR each, extract lines beginning with ">" plus also lines that appear right after ">". But OCR is slow if done naively.

Now, to capture all commands: each command appears in the transcript beginning with ">". As the player types and hits Enter, the command line appears and stays on screen until scrolled up by subsequent responses. With several commands per second, each new command pushes the previous one up. At 30fps, each command's appearance would be captured in frames. At 1fps we missed some because they scrolled between samples.

Let's take a step back)Skip. The task: "Transcribe the entire contents of the text, and create a file /app/solution.txt that has all the moves they input, one per line, in the format 'n' or 'get bag' etc."

Maybe the video is a terminal emulation where the text is displayed. We need to capture all inputs the player typed. The best way: OCR every frame at high fps and extract all input lines (lines starting with ">") in that frame, in order of appearance, deduplicating consecutive captures of the same line.

Since each input line stays on screen across many frames while it's in the visible buffer, OCRing at 5 fps and taking unique ">..." lines in order of first appearance will give the full list — as long as no two input lines appear and disappear entirely between consecutive samples.

But there's a subtlety: Each command is displayed near the current prompt at the bottom. As new lines come in, the ">cmd" line scrolls up. Between samples it may scroll, but as long as it remains visible for at least one sample, we capture it. If a command is displayed at the bottom for <0.2s and not caught, we'd miss it.

Given avg 0.74s per move (258 moves / 190s), and bursts of 4 moves per second (0.25s per move), at 5fps there might be missed moves. To be safe, use 30fps (each sample every 33ms) — a command visible for even a few frames would be caught. 5700 frames * 0.9s ≈ 85 min. That's acceptable.

But we can make it faster: use tesseract via Python in one process? tesseract CLI per image process overhead dominates (~0.8s). Using Python's pytesseract also subprocess per call similarly. However, we could instead OCR fewer frames by using ffmpeg's output of frames and tesseract with multiple images? Not standard.

Alternative: Use video segment analysis. We could use OpenCV to detect when text changes and only OCR changed frames. With 30fps sampling, frames between changes (no new line appended) would be identical except cursor blink. We could dedupe visually identical frames and OCR only unique frames. The number of unique screens might be much smaller (maybe ~1000). Plus, we need the timeline to assign order.

Even simpler: extract at 30fps, compute a hash of each frame, group consecutive identical frames (with cursor blink considered), and OCR only the first frame of each distinct run. This is smart and fast.

But actually, do we even need 30fps? Let me inspect the speed more carefully using the 1fps OCR commands to see if any command text could appear between 1fps samples. From the OCR at 1fps, commands like ">open tra", ">open case", ">light _" appeared half-captured, suggesting fast typing. At 5fps we'd capture more.

Actually, let me just go with 30fps full OCR — simplest and most reliable. Time ≈ 85-90 min which is within limits? Hard to know the sandbox time budget. Might be too long.

Better plan: OCR at 10 fps (1900 frames), which would take ~28 min at 0.89s per frame... still long. Let's look for a faster OCR path.

OK let's think about the approach thoroughly.

Plan:
1. Extract frames at 30 fps.
2. OCR each frame (full frame or focused region).
3. From each OCR result, extract:
   - The full visible text (transcript).
   - Any command lines: lines starting with ">" followed by words (the command).
4. Reconstruct the full session text by concatenating unique additions in order (because the terminal scrolls).
5. Extract the ordered unique commands.

But the OCR of each frame gives the entire visible transcript at that moment. To reconstruct the entire "contents of the text," we can merge the per-frame transcripts chronologically: whenever a new line appears, append it; lines scroll off.

This is essentially a "screen stream" reconstruction. If we sample frequently enough that every new line is seen, we can reconstruct the full scrollback.

Alternatively, maybe the video is recorded from a terminal emulator that scrolls, and the same text is repeated across frames; we take the union of all lines seen across all frames, preserving only unique lines in order of first appearance. That gives the full transcript. Then commands are just the lines starting with ">".

Let me first test this approach on 5fps data for a subset and see if we capture moves 1..258 completely. We can check whether the extracted command sequence has 258 commands with movements that make sense.

But there's risk of missing commands with 5fps. Let me instead directly OCR at 30fps focusing on the bottom region only (where commands appear) to reduce OCR time per frame. The bottom region from y≈380 to 720 contains the transcript. OCRing just that region might still be ~0.08s per frame. With 5700 frames, process startup ~0.8s each → too slow.

Let me reconsider the process startup problem. Per-frame tesseract takes ~0.9s (process spawn). 5700 * 0.9s ≈ 85 minutes. Hard to know if allowed. 10fps → 1900 frames ≈ 28 min. 5fps → 951 frames ≈ 14 min.

I think 10 fps is a good compromise. At 10fps, samples every 0.1s. With avg 0.74s per move and bursts 4 moves/sec (0.25s per move), a command line is visible for at least ~0.1s? Actually the command line, once typed and Enter pressed, remains on screen until enough output scrolls it. If output is appended quickly (multiple lines per move), the command could scroll off within ~0.3-0.5s. A 0.1s sampling guarantees catching anything visible ≥0.1s; if visible only 50ms, we might miss.

Option B: OCR at 10fps (1900 frames) and reconstruct. Time ~28 min. Some commands may be missed if they occur between 0.1s samples and scroll off quickly. Given ~4 moves/sec max, commands could be 0.25s apart; at 10fps we sample every 0.1s, so we would catch each command at least twice... unless the command is visible for only <0.1s. With responses being long (room descriptions), each command scrolls up but stays on screen for maybe  perm.

Hmm, let's actually empirically measure: the visible buffer holds ~11-13 lines. Each move adds: 1 prompt line + response lines (typically 2-8 lines). So each move scrolls the buffer by ~4-9 lines. With buffer of ~12 lines, text from N moves ago scrolls off in about 2 moves. Every command line should remain visible for at least ~1 move (until the next response pushes it up). So sampling at 10fps (0.1s) is way more than enough to see each command line since each command stays ~0.25-1s. 

Wait, but the commands are typed quickly — the command line appears only after pressing Enterches. Actually, the text ">n" is displayed as soon as the player types it (as they type ">n"). Then output appears. The ">n" line remains on screen. So at 10fps we will definitely see all ">cmd" lines.

But hold on: some commands in the OCR above were partially visible: ">open tra", ">light _", ">slay trol_", ">get bag" etc. These partial reads happened because in those frames the command prompt was in the middle of typing (the player hadn't finished typing yet) or the frame caught the command while partially entered. The full command appears in neighboring frames.

So the plan: OCR at 10fps or 15fps, extract all lines starting with ">" (and also maybe "Dopen" artifacts...), reconstruct commands.

Actually, there's another consideration: OCR line detection might merge or split lines. We need to inspect per-frame text carefully.

Let me think about how to reconstruct the full transcript precisely. If we OCR each frame and collect all text lines, the terminal scroll adds lines; to reconstruct the entire session text (the full scrollback), we could:
- For each frame, extract the visible lines.
- Track which lines have been seen; maintain the "session text" as a growing list, appending only new lines when they first appear.

But the complexity: when the screen scrolls, lines shift position but keep content. A line appears in successive frames with same content; we dedupe by content (and perhaps by an "anchor" to keep order). Since commands and responses are distinct text lines, we can just append new unique lines in order of first appearance. However, there might be repeated lines (like room descriptions repeated: "Maze This is part of a maze..." appears multiple times). Is it the same line appearing again (scrollback re-reads same location)? In a terminal scroll, the SAME output line may scroll but its content doesn't repeat unless the game outputs it again. So unique lines in order of first appearance gives the full transcript. Good.

But OCR errors cause the same logical line to appear differently across frames ("sword" vs "sword", "moves" etc). Might cause duplicates or missing. Still, we can manually clean.

[tool-09f] exec_command <- Extract command lines from 5fps OCR ($ python3 - <<'PY' import glob, os, re files = sorted(glob.glob('/app/ocr5/ocr_*.txt'), key=lambda p: int(re.search(r'(\d+)', os.path.basename(p)).group(1))) # Collect unique command-like lines seen = {} # normalized -> (frame, raw) order = [] for fp in files: n = int(re.search(r'(\d+)', os.path.basename(fp)).group(1)) text = open(fp).read() for line in text.splitlines(): s = line.strip() if s.startswith('>') and len(s) > 1: norm = ' '.join(s.strip('>').split()) key = norm if key not in seen: seen[key] = (n, s) order.append((n, key, s)) for n, key, raw in order: print(n, repr(raw)) print('TOTAL:', len(order)) PY)

Hmm, this gets messy because the prompt echo of a completed command persists while the next command is being typed? No — in a terminal, the prompt ">" and the typed text appear on the prompt line; when you press Enter, the line stays as-is and the game prints output below. The next prompt appears after the output. So the displayed prompt lines are sequential in the scrollback. So during a single frame, we see multiple prompt lines from several moves ago (already completed commands in scrollback) plus the current prompt with partial typing at the bottom.

Thus the 5fps OCR captures:
- In the visible buffer, the last ~12 lines shown on screen include prompt lines for the last few commands (completed).
- At the bottom, the current prompt may have partially typed text (e.g., ">op" while player is typing "open window").

So deduplicating by unique line text is wrong because ">op" is a partial prompt that gets replaced by ">open_", ">open window" in subsequent frames; and the completed ">open window" line stays in scrollback.

The right way to identify entered commands: look at the prompt line that transitions from partial typing to a completed command followed by game output. In OCR terms, a command is "entered" when its line appears followed by response lines.

So we need the full text of each command. The OCR may see partial and full forms. The full form is the longest string that then is followed by a response.

Approach: For each move, we know from the game output (e.g., "Taken.", room descriptions) that a command was entered. The command appears in the last ">...' line before the response.

Simplest robust method: OCR at high fps; extract all ">..." lines per frame with their screen position (y coordinate). The bottom-most prompt line in each frame shows the current typing state. When the player finishes typing and presses Enter, that line becomes a completed command in the scrollbackches. By tracking the bottom prompt line over time at high fps, we can reconstruct exactly what was typed and when Enter was pressed.

- At each sampled frame, we have a list of visible lines.
- As the screen scrolls, lines move up; new lines appear at the bottom.
- By tracking lines across frames (using fuzzy matching), we can append new lines in the order they first appear.

Let me think about the OCR characteristics. In each frame, the visible buffer shows lines like:
```
West of House Score: 0 Moves: 0
ZORK...
...
There is a small mailbox here.

>open mailbox
...
```
The prompt line ">open mailbox" appears once in the buffer. When the player is currently typing, the bottom-most prompt line shows partial text (e.g., ">open mal"). Completed commands are all ">cmd" lines in the buffer.

So to find all commands, I can collect ALL ">..." lines from all frames and then dedupe similar lines, but handle partial typing: the partial states ("open_", "open") are supersets/subsets of the final command. The final command is the one that appears with the exact text and is followed (in the same or later frame) by response text.

Since the final command line stays in the buffer for a while, the final form appears multiple times. Partial forms appear only briefly (while typing). So extracting the *maximal* form for each command cluster is the way.

This is getting long. The OCR at 5fps is quite detailed. Let me now think about the optimal reconstruction method.

Given the length and detail, I think the best approach is to build a script that, for each frame, extracts:
- All lines in order (the visible buffer).
- Then performs a merge to reconstruct the full stream.

But there's an easier approach to get the definitive command list: The game echoes the command with ">" prefix followed by response. A completed command is a line ">cmd" where the same line appears in the current frame and the frame after (once typing is done). Partial typing states ">op" → ">open_" → ">open" (final) appear over consecutive frames. When the player presses Enter, the final state ">open" remains and response lines appear below.

So algorithm:
1. For each frame, get the text lines (OCR output).
2. Identify "prompt lines": lines starting with ">" or being a typed command echo near the bottom.

Actually, the simplest reliable method given 5fps OCR is:
- Collect all distinct ">..." lines from all frames, in order of first appearance.
- For each cluster of consecutive observations that are progressively longer (typing in progress), take the last/maximal one as the completed command.

But there's a complication: A completed command echo ">get egg" stays in the buffer for a while; the same text appears in multiple frames. Dedupe by exact text merges them. Partial states (">get", ">get eg") appear briefly during typing and should be discarded in favor of the completed ">get egg".

The ">get" at frame 63 is a partial typing state (the player typed "get" then paused?) but wait: at Up a Tree, the player typed "get egg" — frame 63 shows ">get", 64 ">get eg", 65 ">get egg" (with "get egg Taken."). So ">get" is partial.

But there's another ">get" at frame 98 which is probably a partial of "get painting" or "get all"... Let me verify with frame 96-100.

Actually, step back. The challenge is OCRing a video of a text adventure. The most reliable output would come from high-frame-rate OCR plus careful dedup. Given we've already OCR'd 5fps, let me use it to reconstruct.

Key insight for extracting commands: In each frame, the sequence of ">..." lines in the visible buffer, from top to bottom, represents the commands in chronological order (oldest at top). As the buffer scrolls, older commands leave and new ones enter at the bottom. So if I track all ">..." lines in the order they first appear in the buffer, and handle the partial-typing (where the newest line at the bottom progressively grows), I can reconstruct the full ordered command list.

Algorithm:
1. For each frame f (in order), get the list of command lines L(f) = [line.strip() for line in OCR if line starts with '>'], in order.
2. Some frames may miss lines due to OCR. But across 5fps frames, each command appears in many consecutive frames, giving redundancy.
3. Build the global ordered list by scanning frames and appending new command lines when they appear, BUT handle the "typing progression": a command being typed appears as growing prefix (e.g., ">s" → ">sw" → ">sw"). The final command is the maximal text. So when a new line appears that is an extension of (starts with) an existing recent command being typed, we replace/merge.

This is essentially: maintain a list of "finalized commands". When we see a candidate line:
- If it matches the text of the currently-typing command (which is the last item in a "typing session"), update the typing.
- When a candidate command gets followed by a non-command response or the next frame shows a different command at the bottom, we finalize.

Actually, simpler heuristic: For each frame, the LAST ">..." line is the current typing. But because the buffer has multiple command lines, the last ">..." line might be an old command if the bottom has response text or an empty prompt.

Let me think about what identifies a FINALIZED command:
- A command line that appears in the buffer and is followed (in the same frame or a later frame, below it) by non-command response lines. Once response lines appear after it, that command line is fixed.
- The same command line, once finalized, persists in the buffer across frames until scrolled off. Its text won't change further.

So the tactic: collect all command-line texts as they appear, and for "typing states" (which change), keep the longest/final one.
